Online Tensor Learning: Computational and Statistical Trade-offs, Adaptivity and Optimal Regret
Jingyang Li, Jian-Feng Cai, Yang Chen, Dong Xia

Abstract
Large tensor learning algorithms are typically computationally expensive and require storing a vast amount of data. In this paper, we propose a unified online Riemannian gradient descent (oRGrad) algorithm for tensor learning, which is computationally efficient, consumes much less memory, and can handle sequentially arriving data while making timely predictions. The algorithm is applicable to both linear and generalized linear models. If the time horizon T T is known, oRGrad achieves statistical optimality by choosing an appropriate fixed step size. We find that noisy tensor completion particularly benefits from online algorithms by avoiding the trimming procedure and ensuring sharp entry-wise statistical error, which is often technically challenging for offline methods. The regret of oRGrad is analyzed, revealing a fascinating trilemma concerning the computational convergence rate, statistical error, and regret bound. By selecting an appropriate constant step size, oRGrad achieves an O ⁡ ( T 1 / 2 ) O(T^{1/2}) regret. We then introduce the adaptive-oRGrad algorithm, which can achieve the optimal O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) O(\log T) regret by adaptively selecting step sizes, regardless of whether the time horizon is known. The adaptive-oRGrad algorithm can attain a statistically optimal error rate without knowing the horizon. Comprehensive numerical simulations corroborate our theoretical findings. We show that oRGrad significantly outperforms its offline counterpart in predicting the solar F10.7 index with tensor predictors that monitor space weather impacts.



1
Introduction

The technological revolution in data collection and processing over the past decades has made large tensor data available in diverse fields, such as international trade flow
(
Cai et al. 2022a
;
Lyu et al. 2021
)
, malaria parasite gene networks
(
Jing et al. 2021
;
Larremore et al. 2013
)
, the BHL dataset
(
Lyu and Xia 2022
;
Mai et al. 2021
)
, EEG datasets
(
Hu et al. 2020
;
Huang et al. 2022
)
, hyperspectral imaging
(
Li and Li 2010
)
, medical image analysis
(
Gandy et al. 2011
;
Wang and Li 2020
)
, multi-way recommender systems
(
Bi et al. 2018
)
, spatio-temporal analysis
(
Chen et al. 2020
;
Liu et al. 2022
)
, 4D scanning transmission electron microscopy
(
Han et al. 2022b
)
, and hypergraph network analysis
(
Ke et al. 2019
)
.

Low-rank tensor models assume that the observed data are sampled from a statistical model characterized by an
unknown
but
low-rank
tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
of size
d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}
. A tensor is considered low-rank if it is the sum of a few
rank-one
tensors, which can be understood as a generalization of low-rank matrix (see the formal definition in Section
2
). The low-rank assumption substantially reduces the model complexity from
d^{\ast}:=d_{1}\cdots d_{m}
to
O(md_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})
, where
d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}:=\max_{j\in[m]}d_{j}
. The primary goal in these models is to estimate the latent tensor, a procedure known as
low-rank tensor learning
. There is a vast literature studying the computational and statistical aspects of low-rank tensor learning. Tensor linear regression aims to recover a low-rank tensor from a collection of linear measurements and their respective (noisy) outcomes. Computationally efficient estimators are attainable via importance sketching
(
Zhang et al. 2020
)
, projected gradient descent
(
Chen et al. 2019a
)
, Burer-Monteiro type gradient descent
(
Han et al. 2022b
)
, Riemannian gradient descent
(
Shen et al. 2022
)
, scaled gradient descent
(
Tong et al. 2021
)
, and nuclear-norm penalized least squares via matricization
(
Mu et al. 2014
)
. Tensor regression has been further investigated in generalized linear models to handle categorical responses
(
Cai et al. 2022a
;
Han et al. 2022b
;
Chen et al. 2019a
)
. Tensor completion refers to the problem of reconstructing a tensor by observing only a small fraction of its (noisy) entries. An incomplete list of representative works include convex programming via minimizing tensor nuclear norm
(
Yuan and Zhang 2016
)
, vanilla or scaled gradient descent
(
Cai et al. 2019
;
Tong et al. 2021
)
, alternating minimization
(
Jain and Oh 2014
)
, Grassmannian gradient descent
(
Xia and Yuan 2019
)
, higher-order orthogonal iterations
(
Xia et al. 2021
)
, sum-of-squares hierarchy
(
Barak and Moitra 2016
)
, and Riemannian gradient descent
(
Kressner et al. 2014
;
Cai et al. 2022b
)
. Binary tensor learning or one-bit tensor completion aims to estimate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
from binary entry-wise observations
(
Wang and Li 2020
;
Cai et al. 2022a
)
.

The aforementioned works focused on
offline learning
, where a set of tensorial data is collected and a low-rank tensor model is fitted. This usually involves an iterative algorithm that uses all the data simultaneously, during which the data itself remains static. The offline learning framework has several limitations. For many applications, data are revealed sequentially, requiring timely predictions. A typical example is large-scale recommendation system
(
Chang et al. 2017
;
Zhang et al. 2019
;
Davidson et al. 2010
;
Linden et al. 2003
)
, where vast amounts of user-related feedback are gathered every minute. User preferences drift over time, affecting prediction accuracy. For example, a tweet that was popular last month may be less popular now. See also the application of predicting the solar index F10.7 in Section
8.4
, where we show that the online tensor learning method can significantly outperform its offline counterpart in prediction accuracy.
Designing an algorithm that updates in real-time with newly-arrived data, referred to as
online learning
, is crucial. Another major issue with offline learning is the high computation and storage cost. The size of a tensor increases exponentially with its order. In contrast, an online learning algorithm
(
Langford et al. 2009
)
updates in real-time using only one or a few observations, which are discarded afterwards, making it applicable to large-scale systems. Although this may seem wasteful, online methods can achieve performance comparable to offline counterparts that use all data together. This phenomenon has been observed in streaming principal component analysis (PCA)
(
Shamir 2016
;
Jain et al. 2016
;
Allen-Zhu and Li 2017
)
, online sparse linear regression
(
Langford et al. 2009
;
Foster et al. 2016
;
Fan et al. 2018
)
, and online sparse PCA
(
Yang and Xu 2015
)
.

An online algorithm based on convex programming was proposed by
Meka et al. 2008
for rank minimization over a polyhedral set. An online Riemannian gradient descent algorithm, equipped with an approximated SVD as the retraction, for estimating a low-rank matrix under a general loss function was studied in
Shalit et al. 2012
. However, theoretical guarantees of algorithmic convergence and statistical performance are missing in both
Meka et al. 2008
and
Shalit et al. 2012
. Exact matrix completion was studied by
Jin et al. 2016
, showing that a simple online gradient descent algorithm provably recovers the matrix within
\tilde{O}(r^{2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})
iterations, where
r
denotes the rank.
A closely related study by
Han et al. 2022a
examines the online stochastic gradient descent algorithm for addressing the matrix contextual bandit problem. The study establishes the estimation error rate and proposes an inference procedure under the low-rank regression model. In
Ge et al. 2015
, the authors demonstrated a strict saddle property for noiseless orthogonal tensor decomposition, showing that a randomly initialized stochastic gradient algorithm successfully recovers the latent tensor within a polynomial number of iterations. Their online tensor-based method is further utilized in
Huang et al. 2015
to learn latent variable models with applications in community detection and topic modeling. Unfortunately, the statistical performance of their algorithm is still not provided in
Huang et al. 2015
. See also
Yu et al. 2015
;
Li et al. 2018
;
Mairal et al. 2010
;
De Sa et al. 2015
and references therein.

The statistical understanding of online methods is largely unknown for most popular low-rank models. While online gradient descent is effective in noiseless matrix and tensor decomposition
(
Ge et al. 2015
;
Jin et al. 2016
)
, it is well-recognized that random noise significantly alters the optimization landscape, causing even offline gradient descent algorithms to often become trapped in locally optimal solutions
(
Arous et al. 2019
)
. This suggests that the dynamics of online tensor learning algorithms may be drastically different under random noise. Intuitively, each time a new noisy observation arrives, the online algorithm faces a dilemma: assign more weight for aggressive updates, which causes noise to accumulate quickly, or assign less weight for slower updates, allowing noise to accumulate more slowly. To the best of our knowledge, there is a lack of online algorithms for learning low-rank tensors under generalized linear models. This gap exists even for matrix cases. The primary advantage of online learning algorithms is their ability to reduce computational and storage costs. It remains unclear whether online methods offer additional benefits, particularly regarding statistical performance and algorithm design. Surprisingly, we find that online methods can achieve results considered extremely challenging for offline methods.

Regret measures the prediction performance of an online algorithm. In a seminal paper,
Hazan et al. 2007
showed that the online gradient descent (OGD) algorithm achieves
O(\log T)
regret for (strongly) convex programming, where
T
is the time horizon. Regret analysis of online tensor learning is more challenging due to the non-convexity caused by the rank constraint.
Shi et al. 2023
and
Zhou et al. 2020
derived
O(T^{1/2})
for low-rank tensor bandit. Remarkably, we demonstrate that an adaptive online tensor learning algorithm can achieve the optimal
O(\log T)
regret.

Our contributions

We investigate online tensor learning within a general framework, covering both linear and generalized linear models. Compared to
Jin et al. 2016
and
Ge et al. 2015
, our method accommodates noisy and discrete-type observations, making it more suitable for statistical applications. We propose a computationally fast
o
nline algorithm based on
R
iemannian
grad
ient descent, referred to as the
oRGrad
algorithm. At time
t
, with the current estimate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
, a new observation
\mathfrak{D}_{t}
arrives, incurring a one-time loss
\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D}_{t})
. Then, oRGrad updates the estimate as follows:

{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t+1}\leftarrow\textsf{Retraction}\big({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-\eta\cdot\nabla_{\textsf{R}}\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D}_{t})\big),

(1)

where
\eta
is the step size,
\nabla_{\textsf{R}}
represents the Riemannian gradient, and
\textsf{Retraction}(\cdot)
projects a tensor onto the Riemannian manifold. If
\nabla_{\textsf{R}}
is replaced with the vanilla gradient, (
1
) coincides with projected gradient descent
(
Chen and Wainwright 2015
;
Chen et al. 2019a
)
. The Riemannian gradient is low-rank, which significantly speeds up the computation of subsequent retractions
(
Cai et al. 2022a
;
Kressner et al. 2014
;
Zheng et al. 2022
)
. Our oRGrad algorithm uses higher order singular value decomposition (HOSVD, see the formal definition in Section
2
) for the retraction step. More recently,
Luo and Zhang 2022
demonstrated that Riemannian optimization is robust to over-specification of the underlying ranks.

To summarize, we make the following contributions.

(a)

We demonstrate that, under suitable conditions, after
t
iterations, oRGrad, with high probability, achieves an estimate (
informally
) satisfying

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq 2(1-\eta)^{t}\cdot\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}+O\big(\eta\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\sigma^{2}\big),

(2)

where
\eta\in(0,1)
is a
fixed
step size,
\overline{\textsf{dof}}
describes the degree of freedom for
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
,
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
denotes the initial estimate, and
\sigma
represents the noise level. The contraction rate is independent of the condition number of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
, which is an advantage of the Riemannian gradient descent algorithm. This contraction dynamic is established for the various tensor models discussed above. The
computational and statistical trade-off
is observed from (
2
). A larger step size
\eta
leads to faster convergence of oRGrad, but the final output has a larger statistical error. If the time horizon
T
is known, one can choose
\eta_{\textsf{stat}}:=C_{0}T^{-1}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
for a large
C_{0}>0
. Then the final output, with high probability, achieves a statistical error
O_{m}(T^{-1}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\sigma^{2}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})
, under mild conditions. Amazingly, it matches the best rate, up to a logarithm factor, in the minimax sense, attainable by offline methods even if all the sequential observations are presented at once.

(b)

Noisy tensor (or matrix) completion benefits significantly from oRGrad. A major challenge in designing tensor completion algorithms is maintaining the so-called incoherence (see Section
4
for a formal definition). This is especially true for Riemannian optimization, which often involves SVD at each iteration. Existing algorithms
(
Cai et al. 2022a
;
Cai et al. 2022b
)
require additional trimming procedures after step (
1
). By exploiting the online nature of oRGrad and a spectral representation tool from
Xia 2021
, we can directly show that
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
is already incoherent, bypassing the trimming step.
Another surprising advantage of the online method is that we can easily derive a sharp upper bound for the entry-wise error
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
, which is highly valuable for statistical inference
(
Chen et al. 2019b
;
Xia and Yuan 2021
)
in low-rank models. Deriving the entry-wise error is notoriously challenging for noisy tensor (matrix) completion, where prior works
(
Chen et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2021
)
often rely on a complicated leave-one-out framework to analyze algorithmic dynamics finely. Our results suggest that, with a warm initialization,
\tilde{O}_{m}(d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})
randomly sampled entries suffice to complete the whole tensor. In contrast, most prior works still require a sample size
\tilde{O}_{m}\big((d^{\ast})^{1/2}\vee d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\big)
even with a warm initialization. Table
1
presents the advantages of oRGrad in tensor completion.

(
Yuan and Zhang 2016
)

(
Xia and Yuan 2019
)

\tilde{O}_{m}(d^{m/2})

N/A

Scaled GD

(
Tong et al. 2021
)

\tilde{O}_{m}(d^{m/2})

(

)

Total flops of oRGrad can be further reduced to
O(d\log\epsilon^{-1})
if an appropriate retraction is chosen. Here, we opt to HOSVD as retraction for technical convenience, but it leads to
O(d^{2}\log\epsilon^{-1})
total flops.
