:
Comparison with existing algorithms on tensor completion
assuming a warm initialization is provided
. Here the latent
m
-th order tensor is of size
d\times\cdots\times d
whose ranks are assumed to be constants. The total flops is calculated when an
\epsilon
-accurate estimate is reached, i.e.,
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{-1}\leq\epsilon
. Note that
Cai et al. 2019
deals with a CP-format tensor, whereas others focus on Tucker-format tensors.

(c)

The merits of oRGrad are further demonstrated in binary tensor learning. Assuming a logistic link, we show that oRGrad naturally preserves incoherence, eliminating the need for additional trimming. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first result of its kind. Finally, with a properly chosen step size relative to the time horizon, a minimax optimal error rate (up to logarithmic factors) can be achieved.

(d)

We show that oRGrad, equipped with a fixed step size
\eta
, achieves a regret
O(\eta^{-1}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\eta T\overline{\textsf{dof}}\sigma^{2})
, highlighting a trilemma concerning the computational convergence, statistical error, and regret. Compared to the statistically optimal value
\eta_{\textsf{stat}}\asymp T^{-1}
, the regret bound is minimized at
\eta_{\textsf{regret}}\asymp T^{-1/2}
, allowing oRGrad to achieve the
O(T^{1/2})
regret. We then propose the adaptive-oRGrad algorithm, which selects step sizes adaptively, and show that adaptive-oRGrad attains the
optimal

O(\log T)
regret. To the best of our knowledge, our result represents the first logarithmic regret in the high-dimensional online learning literature. Moreover, we show that the adaptive-oRGrad algorithm can achieve statistical optimality or the optimal
O(\log T)
regret if the step sizes are adaptively selected, even when the time horizon is unknown.

(e)

We apply the oRGrad algorithm to predict the solar index F10.7 using ionospheric total electron content data. Compared to its offline counterpart, our numerical experiments demonstrate that oRGrad achieves significantly higher prediction accuracy.

2
Methodology

2.1
Background and notations

We use calligraphic-font bold-face letters (e.g.
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}},{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}
) to denote tensors, bold-face capital letters (e.g.
{\bm{T}},{\bm{X}}
) for matrices, bold-face lower-case letters (e.g.
{\bm{t}},{\bm{x}}
) for vectors and blackboard bold-faced letters (e.g.
{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{M}}
) for sets. We use square brackets with subscripts (e,g.
[{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}]_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}
) to represent corresponding entries of tensors.
Denote
\|\cdot\|_{\rm{F}}
the Frobenius norm of tensors, and
\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{p}}
the
\ell_{p}
-norm of tensors for
0<p\leq+\infty
. Specifically,
\|{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
represents the largest magnitude of the entries of
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}
. We denote
C,C_{1},C_{2},c,c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
some absolute constants whose actual values might vary at different appearances. For nonnegative
A
and
B
, the notation
A\lesssim B
(equivalently,
B\gtrsim A
) means that there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that
A\leq CB
;
A\asymp B
is equivalent to
A\lesssim B
and
A\gtrsim B
, simultaneously. For any
1\leq\alpha\leq 2
, the Orlicz norm of a random variable is defined by
\|X\|_{\psi_{\alpha}}:=\min\{C>0:{\mathbb{E}}\exp\{|X/C|^{\alpha}\}\leq 2\}
.

An
m
-th order tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
means that its
j
-th dimension has size
d_{j}
.
The
j
-th
matricization

{\mathcal{M}}_{j}(\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{j}\times d_{j}^{-}}
with
d_{j}^{-}:=d^{*}/d_{j}
. We also denote
(\cdot)_{(j)}:={\mathcal{M}}_{j}(\cdot)
for simplicity. If
m=3
, we have
[{\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}})\big]_{i_{1},(i_{2}-1)d_{3}+i_{3}}=[{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}]_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}
for
\forall i_{j}\in[d_{j}]
. The collection

\textsf{rank}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}):=\big(\textsf{rank}({\mathcal{M}}_{1}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}})),\cdots,\textsf{rank}({\mathcal{M}}_{m}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}))\big)^{\top}

is called the
Tucker ranks
of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Given a matrix
{\bm{W}}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p_{j}\times d_{j}}
for any
j\in[m]
, the mode-
j
marginal product, denoted by
\times_{j}
, between
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
and
{\bm{W}}_{j}
is defined by

[{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\times_{j}{\bm{W}}_{j}]_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}}:=\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{d_{j}}[{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}]_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j-1},k,i_{j+1},\cdots,i_{m}}\cdot[{\bm{W}}_{j}]_{i_{j},k},\ \forall i_{j^{\prime}}\in[d_{j^{\prime}}]\textrm{ for }j^{\prime}\neq j;\forall i_{j}\in[p_{j}].

If
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
has Tucker ranks
{\bm{r}}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{m})^{\top}
, there exist
{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{1}\times\cdots\times r_{m}}
and
{\bm{U}}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{j}\times r_{j}}
satisfying
{\bm{U}}_{j}^{\top}{\bm{U}}_{j}={\bm{I}}_{r_{j}}
for all
j\in[m]
such that
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}={\bm{\mathcal{C}}}\cdot({\bm{U}}_{1},\cdots,{\bm{U}}_{m}):={\bm{C}}\times_{1}{\bm{U}}_{1}\times_{2}\cdots\times_{m}{\bm{U}}_{m}
, known as Tucker decomposition. More details of tensor algebra can be found in
Kolda and Bader 2009
.

Define
r^{*}=r_{1}\cdots r_{m}
,
d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}:=\max_{i\in[m]}d_{i}
, and
d_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}:=\min_{i\in[m]}d_{i}
. Let
{\bm{d}}:=(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m})
. Denote
{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
the collection of all
m
-way tensors of size
d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}
whose Tucker ranks are at most
{\bm{r}}
.
Let
\overline{\textsf{dof}}:=r^{*}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}r_{i}
be the degree of freedom of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
.

2.2
Generalized low-rank tensor learning

A collection of tensorial data
\mathfrak{D}:=\{\mathfrak{D}_{t}:t=0,1,\cdots,T-1\}
is sequentially observed, where we assume for now that the time horizon
T
is known. The scenario where
T
is unknown requires a more involved treatment and will be specifically investigated in Section
7
. The sequence
\{\mathfrak{D}_{t}\}_{t\leq T-1}
is i.i.d. sampled from a distribution characterized by an
unknown
tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}
with ranks
{\bm{r}}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{m})
. Here,
r_{j}\ll d_{j}
. Without loss of generality, let
\mathfrak{D}_{t}=({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},Y_{t})
, where
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
is a covariate tensor of size
d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}
and
Y_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}
denotes its corresponding response.

Let
\ell(\cdot,\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}\times({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}},{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a loss function such that the incurred loss for the
t
-th observation is
\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})
at the estimate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
. Offline tensor learning is formulated as the following non-convex optimization program:

\min_{{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}}\ \mathfrak{L}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}):=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t}).

(3)

Some particularly interesting examples are as follows.

Example 1

(linear regression)
.

The observation satisfies
Y_{t}=\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\rangle+\epsilon_{t}
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the Euclidean inner product, and the noise
\epsilon_{t}
is
centered
sub-Gaussian with proxy variance
\sigma^{2}
. The square loss gives
\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})=(Y_{t}-\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle)^{2}/2
. It finds applications in quantum state tomography
(
Gross et al. 2010
;
Xia and Koltchinskii 2016
)
, spatio-temporal forecasting
(
Arroyo et al. 2021
)
, multi-task learning
(
Chen et al. 2011
)
, and 3D imaging processing
(
Guo et al. 2011
)
, among others.

Example 2

(logistic regression)
.

Conditioned on
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
, the response follows a Bernoulli distribution,
Y_{t}\sim{\rm Ber}(p_{t})
with
p_{t}=f(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast};\sigma\rangle)
, where
f(u;\sigma)=(1+e^{-u/\sigma})^{-1}
is the
logistic link
. The negative log-likelihood as a loss function is given by

\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})=-Y_{t}\log\big(f(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle)\big)-(1-Y_{t})\log\big(1-f(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle)\big).

Example 3

(Poisson regression)
.

Conditioned on
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
, the response
Y_{t}
follows the Poisson distribution
Y_{t}\sim\text{Pois}(I\exp(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\rangle))
, where
I>0
stands for the intensity parameter.
The negative log-likelihood is

\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})=-I^{-1}Y_{t}\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle+\exp(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle).

It finds applications in hyper-spectral imaging
(
Zhang and Ng 2021
)
, positron-emission tomography
(
Ollinger and Fessler 1997
)
, astronomical images
(
Molina 1994
)
, photon-limited imaging
(
Salmon et al. 2014
)
, and online click-through data analysis
(
Shan et al. 2016
)
.

Example 4

(noisy tensor completion)
.

The pair
({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},Y_{t})
represents a random noisy entry of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
. It is often assumed that
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
is uniformly sampled from
\big\{(d^{\ast})^{1/2}{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{\omega}:\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}]\big\}
, where the
\omega
-th entry of
{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{\omega}
is one and all other entries are zero. The observation satisfies
Y_{t}=\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\rangle+\epsilon_{t}
, where the noise
\epsilon_{t}
is
centered
sub-Gaussian with proxy variance
\sigma^{2}
. The square loss is given by
\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})=(Y_{t}-\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle)^{2}/2
. Besides low-rankness, the tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
possess an additional structure known as
incoherence
(see Section
4
for more details). Tensor completion has been studied by
Barak and Moitra 2016
;
Yuan and Zhang 2016
;
Bi et al. 2018
;
Xia and Yuan 2019
and references therein.

Example 5

(binary tensor learning)
.

The covariate
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
is sampled as in noisy tensor completion, while
Y_{t}
follows a Bernoulli distribution
Y_{t}\sim{\rm Ber}(p_{t})
with
p_{t}=f(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\rangle;\sigma)
being the logistic link. The loss function is typically the negative log-likelihood. Binary tensor learning has been studied by
Cai and Zhou 2013
;
Davenport et al. 2014
;
Wang and Li 2020
;
Han et al. 2022b
;
Cai et al. 2022a
and references therein.

The objective function in (
3
) can be minimized,
at least locally
, by several gradient-type algorithms
(
Xia and Yuan 2019
;
Cai et al. 2019
;
Han et al. 2022b
;
Cai et al. 2022a
;
Kressner et al. 2014
)
. The convergence performance and statistical behavior of these algorithms are well-understood in the offline setting.
At the
t
-th iteration, with the current estimate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
, these algorithms compute the gradient
\nabla\mathfrak{L}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D})
defined over full dataset. Their theoretical investigations crucially rely on a certain concentration property of
\nabla\mathfrak{L}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D})
, for which summing over a large dataset is usually necessary. This property, under suitable conditions, typically guarantees a contraction of error with high probability:

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq(1-\gamma)\cdot\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}+\textsf{Statistical Error},

(4)

where
\gamma\in(0,1)
is a constant independent of dimensions
{\bm{d}}
and horizon
T
.

2.3
Online Riemannian gradient descent

We propose an online tensor learning algorithm called oRGrad, based on Riemannian gradient descent. Unlike the conventional method
(
Kressner et al. 2014
;
Cai et al. 2022a
)
, it computes the gradient using a single observation. When a new observation
\mathfrak{D}_{t}
arrives, with the current estimate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
, the Riemannian gradient
{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{t}}\nabla\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D}_{t})
is calculated. Here,
{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{t}}
denotes the projection on
{\mathbb{T}}_{t}
, the tangent space of the manifold
{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
at
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
.
The Riemannian gradient has a rank of at most
2{\bm{r}}
dimension-wise, facilitating the subsequence computation of low-rank approximation. Closed-form expressions for the Riemannian gradient are well-known; see the Appendix for details. Notably, in some applications, the naive gradient is inherently low-rank. For example, in online tensor completion, it is rank-one, making the Riemannian gradient unnecessary. In such cases, the naive gradient suffices. In this section, we focus on a constant step size and a known time horizon, which facilitates a clear presentation of the dynamics of oRGrad. This serves as the foundation for the adaptive oRGrad algorithm discussed in Section
7
, where step sizes are adaptively chosen and the time horizon is unknown.

ORGrad then updates the estimate to
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+}={\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-\eta\cdot{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{t}}\nabla\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D}_{t})
, which is typically not an element of
{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
. To address this, we apply the higher order singular value decomposition (HOSVD), denoted by
\textsf{HOSVD}_{{\bm{r}}}
, to retract
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+}
back into
{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
. Let
{\bm{U}}_{t,j}^{+}
denote the top-
r_{j}
left singular vectors of
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+})
. Then,

\textsf{HOSVD}_{{\bm{r}}}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+}):={\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+}\cdot\big({\bm{U}}_{t,1}^{+}{\bm{U}}_{t,1}^{+\top},\cdots,{\bm{U}}_{t,m}^{+}{\bm{U}}_{t,m}^{+\top}\big).

Finally, the estimate is updated to
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t+1}\leftarrow\textsf{HOSVD}_{{\bm{r}}}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+})
. The detailed steps of oRGrad are enumerated in Algorithm
1
.
Refer to the Appendix for further details on the computational cost.

Algorithm 1

Online Riemannian gradient descent (oRGrad) –
if time horizon is known

Input:
initialization
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
, time horizon
T
and step size
\eta>0
;

for

t=0,1,2,\ldots,T-1

do

{\bm{\mathcal{G}}}_{t}\leftarrow\nabla\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D}_{t})
;

{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+}\leftarrow{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-\eta\cdot{\mathcal{P}}_{{\mathbb{T}}_{t}}({\bm{\mathcal{G}}}_{t})
;

{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t+1}\leftarrow\textsf{HOSVD}_{{\bm{r}}}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}^{+})
;

end

for

Output:

{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}
.

Online initialization

Note that oRGrad requires an initial estimator
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
. Our theorems require
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}
to be sufficiently close to the true value, as is typically necessary in the literature
(
Han et al. 2022b
;
Cai et al. 2022a
)
. The design of the online initialization algorithms varies depending on specific applications, which will be provided in later sections.

Compared to its offline counterpart, the convergence dynamics of oRGrad exhibit more local volatility. The rationale is simple: at each iteration, the gradient
\nabla\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t},\mathfrak{D}_{t})
is computed on a single datum, which can have remarkably high variance. Consequently, contraction behavior like (
4
) does not hold true for oRGrad. Instead, we can show,
informally
, that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\leq t}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\leq(1-\gamma)\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{\leq t-1}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}+\textsf{Statistical Error}.

(5)

Essentially, contraction only holds in expectation, and the event
\{\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}>\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\}
occurs with a non-negligible probability. The expectation
{\mathbb{E}}_{\leq t}
on LHS of (
5
) is taken w.r.t. the randomness of the
\sigma
-field
{\mathbb{F}}_{t}:=\sigma\big(({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{s},Y_{s}):0\leq s\leq t\big)
. We also denote
{\mathbb{E}}_{t}
as the expectation w.r.t. the randomness of
({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},Y_{t})
, conditioning on the
\sigma
-filed
{\mathbb{F}}_{t-1}
.

The subsequent sections are devoted to rigorously studying the computational and statistical performances of oRGrad for several popular low-rank models. Some notations will frequently appear in these sections. The
signal strength
of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
is defined by
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}:=\min_{k\in[m]}\sigma_{r_{k}}\big({\mathcal{M}}_{k}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast})\big)
, where
\sigma_{r}(\cdot)
denotes the
r
-th largest singular value of a matrix. The
condition number
of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
is defined by
\kappa_{0}:=\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{-1}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
, with
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}=\max_{k\in[m]}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{k}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast})\|
.

3
Online Generalized Tensor Regression

Throughout this section, we focus on the
sub-Gaussian
design under Assumption
1
.

Assumption 1
.

There exist absolute constants
c_{0},C_{0}>0
such that

{\mathbb{E}}\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}\rangle=0,\ c_{0}\leq{\mathbb{E}}\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}\rangle^{2}/\|{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\leq C_{0},\ {\rm and}\ \big\|\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}\rangle\big\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C_{0}\|{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}\|_{\rm F}

for all
t\leq T
and any
{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}
, where
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}}
denotes the Orlicz norm.

Suppose that the loss function is given by

\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})=h(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{T}}},{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}\rangle,Y_{t}),

where the function
h(\cdot,\cdot):{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{Y}}\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
is locally smooth and strongly convex, satisfying Assumption
2
. Here,
{\mathbb{Y}}
denotes the sample space of
Y_{t}
.

Assumption 2
.

For any
\alpha>0
, there exist
\gamma_{\alpha},\mu_{\alpha}>0
such that

\gamma_{\alpha}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}\leq(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})\big(h_{\theta}(\theta_{1},y)-h_{\theta}(\theta_{2},y)\big)\leq\mu_{\alpha}(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}

for all
|\theta_{1}|,|\theta_{2}|\leq\alpha
and
y\in{\mathbb{Y}}
. Here,
h_{\theta}(\theta,y)=\frac{\partial h}{\partial\theta}(\theta,y)
is the partial derivative.

The range of
\alpha
depends on specific applications. For example,
\alpha=\infty
and
\gamma_{\infty}=\mu_{\infty}=1
if
h(\theta,y):=(\theta-y)^{2}/2
.
The following assumption restricts
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}
to a bounded space.

Assumption 3
.

There exists a large constant
C>0
such that
C\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\log^{1/2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq\alpha
. Moreover,

{\mathbb{E}}_{Y_{t}}h_{\theta}(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast},{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}\rangle,Y_{t})=0.

Define
g_{t}(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\rangle):={\mathbb{E}}_{Y_{t}}h_{\theta}^{2}(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\rangle,Y_{t})
.
The statistical accuracy of oRGrad is characterized by

\textsf{Err}_{2}^{2}:=\max_{t=0}^{T}\max_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}g_{t}(\theta).

For example,
\textsf{Err}_{2}^{2}=O(\sigma^{2})
in linear regression. Another important quantity is

\textsf{Err}_{\infty}:=\max_{t=0}^{T}|h_{\theta}(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\rangle,Y_{t})|,

which provides a uniform bound for the noise. For notational simplicity, we denote
\textsf{r}_{\textsf{2}/\infty}:=\textsf{Err}_{2}/\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
-
3
hold, the initialization
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
satisfies
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{m}\mu_{\alpha}^{-1}\gamma_{\alpha}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
for some sufficient small constant
c_{m}>0
. Also assume
C_{0}\textsf{r}_{\textsf{2}/\infty}^{-2}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq\overline{\textsf{dof}}
, the step size
\eta
satisfies

(1)

C_{1}\eta\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\max\bigg\{\textsf{r}_{\textsf{2}/\infty}^{2}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\textsf{r}_{\textsf{2}/\infty}^{2}\mu_{\alpha}^{2}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\mu_{\alpha}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\log^{3}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\bigg\}\leq 1
,

(2)

C_{2}\eta\gamma_{\alpha}\textsf{r}_{\textsf{2}/\infty}^{-4}\log^{3}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq 1
and
C_{3}\eta\mu_{\alpha}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log^{5/2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq 1
,

and the signal strength satisfies

\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}/\textsf{Err}_{\infty}^{2}\geq C_{4}\eta\textsf{r}_{\textsf{2}/\infty}^{-2}\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log^{2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\quad{\rm and}\quad\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}/\textsf{Err}_{2}^{2}\geq C_{m}\eta\gamma_{\alpha}^{-3}\mu_{\alpha}^{2}\overline{\textsf{dof}},

where
C_{0},\ldots,C_{4}>0
are absolute constants, and
c_{m},C_{m}>0
depends only on
m
.
Then, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that, with probability exceeding
1-14Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
, for all
t\leq T
, Algorithm
1
guarantees

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\Big(1-\frac{\eta\gamma_{\alpha}}{8}\Big)^{t}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C\eta\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\textsf{Err}_{2}^{2}.

Theorem
1
demonstrates that the oRGrad algorithm converges linearly. The contraction rate is even independent of the condition number of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
.
The step size balances computational convergence and statistical error: a larger step size speeds up convergence but increases statistical error and demands a higher SNR. In the following subsections, we explore various applications. Due to space constraint, the application of tensor logistic regression is provided in the Appendix.

3.1
Linear regression

Recall that the loss function
h(\theta,y)=\frac{1}{2}(\theta-y)^{2}
, and the noise is sub-Gaussian with proxy variance
\sigma^{2}
. Assumptions
2
and
3
hold with
\alpha=\infty
and
\gamma_{\alpha}=\mu_{\alpha}=1
. Furthermore, we have
\textsf{Err}_{2}^{2}=O(\sigma^{2})
and
{\mathbb{P}}\big(\textsf{Err}_{\infty}\leq C\sigma\log^{1/2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\big)\geq 1-2Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
for some absolute constant
C>0
. This immediately implies the following theorem.

Theorem 2
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds, and the initialization
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
satisfies
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{m}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
for some sufficiently small constant
c_{m}>0
. Assume also
C_{0}\log^{5}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq\overline{\textsf{dof}}
,
\eta
satisfies
C_{1}\eta\overline{\textsf{dof}}\leq 1
, and the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) satisfies

\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq C_{m}\eta\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log^{4}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},

where
C_{0},C_{1}>0
are absolute constants, and
c_{m},C_{m}>0
depend only on
m
. Then, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that, with probability exceeding
1-16Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
, for all
t\leq T
, Algorithm
1
guarantees

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\Big(1-\frac{\eta}{8}\Big)^{t}\cdot\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C\eta\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\sigma^{2}.

(6)

For ease of interpretation, assume
m,r_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\asymp O(1)
and
d_{j}\asymp d
for all
j\in[m]
. The oRGrad algorithm converges the fastest by setting
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{comp}}:=c_{m}d^{-1}\cdot\min\big\{\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}/(\sigma^{2}\log^{4}d),\ 1\}
. However, this aggressive step size results in an estimator that is not even consistent. If the time horizon
T
is known, we can set
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{stat}}:=C_{m}T^{-1}\log d
for a sufficiently large constant
C_{m}>0
depending only on
m
. When
T\leq d^{C_{m}},\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}/\sigma\leq d^{C_{m}}
for a large constant
C_{m}>0
, this leads to

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}=O_{m}\bigg(\sigma^{2}\cdot\frac{\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log d}{T}\bigg),

holding with high probability, which is minimax optimal up to the logarithmic factor. See, e.g.,
Chen et al. 2019a
;
Zhang et al. 2020
;
Han et al. 2022b
. A similar rate is achieved in
Han et al. 2022a
for online inference under matrix linear bandit, requiring stronger SNR and initialization conditions. However, the bandit problem is usually more challenging because finding a tradeoff between exploration and exploitation is crucial.

3.1.1
Online initialization

Our online initialization algorithm consists of two main stages: subspace estimation and core tensor estimation. See Algorithm
2
for the detailed steps, where
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
represent the sample sizes of the first and second stage, respectively.
Each time step in the first stage requires
O(d^{*}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}d_{j}^{2})
storage. In the second stage, the storage requirement is reduced to
O\big((r^{*})^{2}\big)
. The first stage of Algorithm
2
is an online implementation of the second order moment method
(
Xia and Yuan 2019
)
for spectral initialization.
The conventional offline method requires
O\big(T_{0}(d^{*}+1)\big)
storage if
T_{0}
observations are used for initialization.

Algorithm 2

Online initialization for tensor linear regression

Set
{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{0}={\bf 0},{\bm{N}}_{1,0}=\cdots={\bm{N}}_{m,0}={\bf 0}
;

for

t=1,\cdots,T_{1}

do

\triangleright
second order moment

{\bm{N}}_{j,t}={\bm{N}}_{j,t-1}+Y_{t}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{t-1}){\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t})^{\top}+Y_{t}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}){\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{t-1})^{\top},\quad\forall j\in[m]
;

{\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{t}={\bm{\mathcal{M}}}_{t-1}+Y_{t}{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t};

end

for

\widehat{\bm{U}}_{j}=\textsf{SVD}_{r_{j}}(\widehat{\bm{N}}_{j}),\ {\rm where}\ \widehat{\bm{N}}_{j}=\frac{1}{T_{1}(T_{1}-1)}{\bm{N}}_{j,T_{1}},\ \forall j\in[m]
;
\triangleright
spectral method

for

t=T_{1}+1,\cdots,T_{1}+T_{2}

do

\triangleright
Gram matrix

Compute
{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t}={\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}\times_{1}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1}^{\top}\cdots\times_{m}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m}^{\top}
,

{\bm{X}}_{t-T_{1}}={\bm{X}}_{t-T_{1}-1}+\textsf{Vec}({\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t})\textsf{Vec}({\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t})^{\top}\quad{\rm and}\quad{\bm{R}}_{t-T_{1}}={\bm{R}}_{t-T_{1}-1}+Y_{t}{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t}
;

end

for

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}=\textsf{Reshape}(\widehat{\bm{c}},{\bm{r}}),\ {\rm where}\ \widehat{\bm{c}}={\bm{X}}_{T_{2}}^{-1}{\bm{R}}_{T_{2}}
;
\triangleright
linear regression

Output:
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}\times_{1}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1}\cdots\times_{m}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m}.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose the sample sizes satisfy
T_{1}\geq C_{1}r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}\kappa_{0}^{3}\big((d^{*})^{1/2}+d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\kappa_{0}^{3}\big)\log^{2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
and
T_{2}\geq C_{3}(r^{*}+\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})
, and the SNR
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq C_{2}T_{1}^{-1}\big((d^{*})^{1/2}+d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\kappa_{0}^{3}\big)\kappa_{0}\log^{2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
and
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq C_{4}T_{2}^{-1}(r^{*}+\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})
hold for some constant
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}>0
depending only on
c_{m}
.
Then, with probability exceeding
1-8d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
, Algorithm
2
outputs
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
satisfying

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{m}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}},

where
c_{m}\in(0,1)
is a constant depending only on
m
.

Theorem
3
requires a total sample size on the order of
((d^{*})^{1/2}+d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
, which matches the best-known existing results. See, e.g.,
Shen et al. 2022
,
Han et al. 2022b
and references therein.

3.2
Poisson regression

Recall from Example
3
that
Y_{t}|{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}\sim\text{Pois}\big(I\exp(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\rangle)\big)
with
I
being the
intensity
parameter. Assumption
2
holds for the loss
h(\theta,y)=-I^{-1}y\theta+e^{\theta}
with
\gamma_{\alpha}=e^{-\alpha}
and
\mu_{\alpha}=e^{\alpha}
for any
\alpha>0
. Without loss of generality, we assume
I\geq 1
.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
and
3
hold, the initialization satisfies
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{m}e^{-2\alpha}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
for some sufficiently small
c_{m}>0
,

\overline{\textsf{dof}}\geq\tilde{C}_{1}I^{-1}\log^{3}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
with
\tilde{C}_{1}=C_{1}e^{-\alpha}\log^{-2}(1+2I^{-1}e^{-\alpha})
, and

\tilde{C}_{2}\eta\overline{\textsf{dof}}\max\{I\log^{-1}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\log^{5/2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\}\leq 1
with
\tilde{C}_{2}=C_{2}e^{\alpha}\log^{2}(Ie^{-\alpha}\log 2+1)
,
where
C_{1},C_{2}>0
are absolute constants and
c_{m}
depends only on
m
. Then, there exists an absolute constant
C_{3}>0
such that, with probability at least
1-16Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
, for all
t\leq T
, Algorithm
1
guarantees

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\Big(1-\frac{\eta e^{-\alpha}}{8}\Big)^{t}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C_{3}e^{2\alpha}\eta I^{-1}\overline{\textsf{dof}}.

If
m,r_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\alpha\asymp O(1)
and
d_{j}\asymp d
, Theorem
4
requires
\eta\leq\eta_{\textsf{comp}}=\tilde{O}(\frac{1}{dI})
, where
\tilde{O}
hides logarithmic factors. By setting
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{stat}}:=C_{m}T^{-1}e^{\alpha}\log d
with a large enough constant
C_{m}>0
, we get

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}=O_{m}\bigg(e^{3\alpha}I^{-1}\cdot\frac{\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log d}{T}\bigg).

Note that
I^{-1/2}
is often interpreted as noise level
(
Han et al. 2022b
;
Cai et al. 2022a
)
.

3.2.1
Online initialization

We implement online spectral initialization by unfolding a tensor into a matrix of balanced sizes. Define
{\mathcal{I}}:=\arg\min_{{\mathcal{I}}\in[m]}\max\{d_{{\mathcal{I}}},d_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}\}
, where
d_{{\mathcal{I}}}:=\prod_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}}d_{i}
and
d_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}:=d^{\ast}/d_{{\mathcal{I}}}
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{1}\times\cdots\times d_{m}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{D_{1}\times D_{2}}
denote the linear operator which unfolds a tensor into a matrix of size
d_{{\mathcal{I}}}\times d_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}
. Define
r_{{\mathcal{I}}}
and
r_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}
similarly.

Algorithm 3

Online initialization for Poisson regression

Set
{\bm{M}}_{0}={\bf 0}
,
{\mathcal{I}}\subset[m]
,
R=\max\{r_{{\mathcal{I}}},r_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}\}
, and
g(y):=\log\big((y+1/2)/I\big)
;

for

t=1,\cdots,T

do

{\bm{M}}_{t}={\bm{M}}_{t-1}+g(Y_{t}){\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}\big({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}\big)
;

end

for

\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-1}(\widehat{\bm{M}}),
where
\widehat{\bm{M}}=\textsf{SVD}_{R}({\bm{M}}_{T})
;

(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}},\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1},\cdots,\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m})=\textsf{HOSVD}_{{\bm{r}}}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})
;

Output:
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}\times_{1}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1}\cdots\times_{m}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m}
.

Theorem 5
.

Let
D_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}:=\max\{d_{{\mathcal{I}}},d_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}\}
and
R=\max\{r_{{\mathcal{I}}},r_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}\}
. Suppose that
I\geq C_{1,m}R\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{-2}e^{5\alpha}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
and
T\geq C_{2,m}D_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\log(D_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})e^{4\alpha}(1+\alpha)^{2}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{-2}
for some constants
C_{1,m},C_{2,m}>0
depending only on
m
. Then, with probability exceeding
1-5Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-100}
, the output of Algorithm
3
satisfies

\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0,m}e^{-2\alpha}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}

for some small constant
c_{0,m}>0
.

If
d_{j}\asymp d
and
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}},m=O(1)
, Theorem
5
requires a total sample size on the order
d^{\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil}\log d
for a warm initialization. In light of the online nature, Algorithm
3
consumes only
O(d^{\ast})
storage.

4
Online Noisy Tensor Completion

Online noisy tensor completion (Example
4
) aims to reconstruct a tensor by sequentially observing its entries with noise. We assume that
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
is uniformly sampled from the scaled orthonormal basis
\big\{(d^{\ast})^{1/2}{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}_{\omega}:\omega\in[d_{1}]\times\cdots\times[d_{m}]\big\}
and the noise
\epsilon_{t}
is centered sub-Gaussian with proxy variance
\sigma^{2}
. We equip oRGrad with the square loss
\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t}):=(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle-Y_{t})^{2}/2
.

For an orthonormal matrix
{\bm{U}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times r}
satisfying
{\bm{U}}^{\top}{\bm{U}}={\bm{I}}_{r}
, the incoherence of
{\bm{U}}
is defined as

\textsf{Incoh}({\bm{U}}):=(d/r)\max_{i\in[d]}\|{\bm{U}}^{\top}{\bm{e}}_{i}\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}.

The smaller value of
\textsf{Incoh}({\bm{U}})
indicates that the “information” carried by
{\bm{U}}
is more evenly distributed across its rows. Tensor completion becomes an ill-posed problem if some entries are significantly larger than others. The following incoherence assumption rules out these ill-posed scenarios.

Assumption 4
.

Let
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}={\bm{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}\times_{j=1}^{m}{\bm{U}}_{j}^{*}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
with
\{{\bm{U}}_{j}^{*}\}_{j=1}^{m}
being orthonormal matrices. There exists a
\mu>0
such that
\textsf{Incoh}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}):=\max_{j=1}^{m}\textsf{Incoh}({\bm{U}}_{j}^{*})\leq\mu
.

Theorem 6
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
holds, the initialization satisfies
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
,
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
for some
c\in(0,0.5)
,
\textsf{Incoh}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0})\leq 10\mu\kappa_{0}^{2}
, the step size satisfies
C_{0,m}\eta\kappa_{0}^{4m+2}\mu^{2m-1}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}(r^{*})^{2}r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}\leq 1
,
and the signal-to-noise ratio satisfies

(\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}/\sigma)^{2}\geq C_{0,m}\eta\mu^{2m-2}\kappa_{0}^{4m-8}(d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{2}/\overline{\textsf{dof}})(r^{\ast}/r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}})^{2}\log^{2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},

where
C_{0,m}>0
and
c>0
are absolute constants depending on
m
only. Then, there exist absolute constants
C_{1}>0
and
C_{2,m},C_{3,m}>0
depending only on
m
such that, with probability exceeding
1-3Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
, for all
t\leq T
, Algorithm
1
guarantees

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(1-\frac{\eta}{4}\Big)^{t}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C_{1}\eta\overline{\textsf{dof}}\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2,m}\kappa_{0}^{m+2}\mu^{m/2}\Big(1-\frac{\eta}{4}\Big)^{\frac{t}{2}}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\sqrt{\frac{r^{*}}{d^{*}}}+C_{3,m}\kappa_{0}^{m+3}\mu^{m/2}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\eta r^{*}\overline{\textsf{dof}}}{d^{*}}}.

Let us discuss the implications of Theorem
6
. For ease of interpretation, we assume
m,r_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\mu,\kappa_{0}\asymp O(1)
and
d_{j}\asymp d
.

Linear convergence and reduced sample size

Theorem
6
demonstrates that oRGrad converges linearly in both the Frobenius norm and sup-norm. This implies that oRGrad delivers an
\epsilon
-accurate estimate in noiseless tensor completion after
T=\Omega(\eta^{-1}\log\epsilon^{-1})
iterations. By selecting
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{comp}}\asymp d^{-1}
, the required number of iterations becomes
T=\Omega(d\log\epsilon^{-1})
. This represents a significant improvement over existing results. For instance,
Yuan and Zhang 2016
;
Xia and Yuan 2019
;
Xia et al. 2021
;
Tong et al. 2021
;
Cai et al. 2019
all require a sample size condition of
\tilde{\Omega}(d^{m/2})
in offline tensor completion, even when a warm initialization is provided. Nevertheless, we remark that a sample size of
\tilde{\Omega}(d^{m/2})
is still necessary to obtain a desirable initialization.

Theoretical and technical benefits

If the time horizon
T
is known, one can set
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{stat}}\asymp C_{m}T^{-1}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
for some large constant
C_{m}>0
so that oRGrad outputs an estimator with a Frobenius-norm error rate

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=O_{m}\bigg(\sigma^{2}\cdot\frac{\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}}{T}\bigg),

which is minimax optimal up to a logarithmic factor
(
Xia et al. 2021
)
. A surprising theoretical benefit of online algorithm for noisy tensor completion is that one can easily derive the
entry-wise
error rate, which is usually much more challenging yet practically useful. The existing literature on offline noisy matrix/tensor completion
(
Cai et al. 2019
;
Chen et al. 2021
)
often resorts to the rather complicated leave-one-out analysis framework to establish the entry-wise error rate. Benefited from the online nature of oRGrad, we can apply martingale techniques and derive a sharp upper bound for the entry-wise error. Indeed, oRGrad outputs an estimator with a sup-norm error rate

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}^{2}=O_{m}\bigg(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{d^{\ast}}\cdot\frac{\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}}{T}\bigg),

suggesting that the entry-wise error is approximately
1/d^{\ast}
of the Frobenius-norm error. A technical benefit of online algorithm is that it does not require trimming. Maintaining the incoherence condition is crucial in analyzing the convergence of tensor completion algorithms. Most existing literature (except
Cai et al. 2019
;
Chen et al. 2021
) applies an additional trimming procedure to ensure the incoherence property. Interestingly, we can take advantage of martingale techniques and prove the incoherence property during the update of the oRGrad algorithm.

4.1
Initialization

We apply the second-order moment method, originally proposed by
Xia and Yuan 2019
, to obtain a warm initialization for tensor completion (see also
Xia et al. 2021
). This approach is motivated by the fact that
{\bm{U}}_{j}^{*}
constitutes the top
r_{j}
eigenvectors of the following matrix:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\frac{1}{T_{1}(T_{1}-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<i^{\prime}\leq T_{1}}Y_{i}Y_{i^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}){\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i^{\prime}})^{\top}+{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i^{\prime}}){\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i})^{\top}).

The detailed implementation is provided in Algorithm
4
. Given
\tilde{\bm{U}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times r}
, we define
\textsf{Trim}(\tilde{\bm{U}},\mu)=\bar{\bm{U}}(\bar{\bm{U}}^{\top}\bar{\bm{U}})^{-1/2}
, where
[\bar{\bm{U}}]_{i:}=[\tilde{\bm{U}}]_{i:}\cdot\min\big\{1,(\mu r/d)^{1/2}/\|[\tilde{\bm{U}}]_{i:}\|_{\ell_{2}}\big\}
.

Algorithm 4

Initialization for tensor completion

Set
{\bm{X}}_{0}={\bf 0}
and collect data
\{{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T_{1}}
;
\triangleright
spectral initialization

Compute
\tilde{\bm{N}}_{j}:=\frac{1}{T_{1}(T_{1}-1)}\sum_{1\leq i<i^{\prime}\leq T_{1}}Y_{i}Y_{i^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}){\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i^{\prime}})^{\top}+{\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i^{\prime}}){\mathcal{M}}_{j}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{i})^{\top})
;

Trimming and SVD:
\widehat{\bm{U}}_{j}=\textsf{Trim}(\tilde{\bm{U}}_{j},\mu)
where
\tilde{\bm{U}}_{j}=\textsf{SVD}_{r_{j}}(\tilde{\bm{N}}_{j})
;

for

t=T_{1}+1,\cdots,T_{1}+T_{2}

do

\triangleright
core tensor initialization

Compute
{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t}={\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}\times_{1}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1}^{\top}\cdots\times_{m}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m}^{\top}

{\bm{X}}_{t-T_{1}}={\bm{X}}_{t-T_{1}-1}+\textsf{Vec}({\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t})\textsf{Vec}({\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t})^{\top}
and
{\bm{R}}_{t-T_{1}}={\bm{R}}_{t-T_{1}-1}+Y_{t}{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}_{t}
;

end

for

\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}=\textsf{Reshape}(\widehat{\bm{c}},{\bm{r}})
where
\widehat{\bm{c}}={\bm{X}}_{T_{2}}^{-1}{\bm{R}}_{T_{2}}
;

Output:
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}\times_{1}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1}\cdots\times_{m}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m}
.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
holds,
T_{1}\geq C_{1}\big((d^{*})^{1/2}+d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}r^{*}r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{1/2}\mu^{m}\kappa_{0}^{3}\big)\allowbreak r^{*}r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{1/2}\mu^{m}\kappa_{0}^{3}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
,
T_{2}\geq C_{3}\mu^{m}r^{*}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
, and
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}/\sigma^{2}\geq C_{4}T_{2}^{-1}\mu^{m}r^{*}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}+C_{2}T_{1}^{-1}\allowbreak\big((d^{*})^{1/2}+d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{1/2}\mu^{m}\kappa_{0}^{3}r^{*}\big)\kappa_{0}r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{1/2}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
for some constant
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}>0
depending only on
m
. Then, with probability exceeding
1-4T_{1}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-100}
, the output of Algorithm
4
satisfies
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{0,m}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
for some small constant
c_{0,m}\in(0,1/2)
.

Theorem
7
shows that
\tilde{\Omega}\big(((d^{*})^{1/2}+d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\big)
randomly sampled entries suffice to provide a warm initialization.

5
Online Binary Tensor Learning

The covariate
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
is sampled in the same manner as in tensor completion (see Section
4
), but the response
Y_{t}
is binary, following a Bernoulli distribution
Y_{t}\sim{\rm Ber}(p_{t})
with
p_{t}=f(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\rangle;\sigma)
. Here,
f(\theta;\sigma)=f(\theta):=(1+e^{-\theta/\sigma})^{-1}
is the logistic link function. The loss function
\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})=h(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{T}}},{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}\rangle,Y_{t})
is the negative log-likelihood
h(\theta,y)=-y\log(f(\theta))-(1-y)\log(1-f(\theta))
.
The following assumption imposes an upper bound on the entrywise magnitude of
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
.

Assumption 5
.

There exist
\mu,\alpha>0
such that
\textsf{Incoh}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*})\leq\mu
and
2C_{0}(r^{*}/r_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})^{1/2}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq\alpha
, where
C_{0}=20^{m/2}\kappa_{0}^{m+1}\mu^{m/2}
.

Define
L_{\alpha}:=\sup_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}f^{\prime}(\theta)\big(f(\theta)(1-f(\theta))\big)^{-1}
and

\displaystyle\gamma_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\min\bigg\{\inf_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}-f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)f(\theta)}{f^{2}(\theta)},\quad\inf_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)(1-f(\theta))+(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}}{(1-f(\theta))^{2}}\bigg\},

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\max\bigg\{\sup_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}-f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)f(\theta)}{f^{2}(\theta)},\quad\sup_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)(1-f(\theta))+(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}}{(1-f(\theta))^{2}}\bigg\}.

Without loss of generality, we focus on the regime where
d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\asymp d_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}},r_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\asymp r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
and fix
\sigma=1
. In this case, we have
L_{\alpha}=1,\gamma_{\alpha}=e^{\alpha}(1+e^{\alpha})^{-2}
and
\mu_{\alpha}=1/4
.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose Assumption
5
holds and the initialization
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}
satisfies
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{m}\gamma_{\alpha}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
for some small constant
c_{m}>0
depending on
m
only. If
C_{1}\eta d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq 1
,
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}\geq C_{2}\eta\max\{\gamma_{\alpha}^{-3},\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
, and
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq(C_{3}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})^{-1}
, where
C_{1}=\max\big\{(\kappa_{0}^{2}\mu)^{m-1}r^{*}/r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}},1\big\}
,
C_{2}=\max\big\{\kappa_{0}^{4m-4}\mu^{2m-2},r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{-1}\kappa_{0}^{2m-4}\mu^{m-2}\big\}\cdot r^{*}r_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{-1}
, and
C_{3}=20^{m}\kappa_{0}^{3m+1}\mu^{\frac{3m}{2}-1}(r^{*})^{3/2}/r_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
, then there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that, with probability exceeding
1-5Td^{-10}
, for all
t\leq T
, Algorithm
1
guarantees

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\Big(1-\frac{1}{4}\eta\gamma_{\alpha}\Big)^{t}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C\eta\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\overline{\textsf{dof}}.

There is an implicit step size requirement in Theorem
8
arising from the conditions of
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
and
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
. Specifically, we require
\eta\leq c_{1}\min\big\{\gamma_{\alpha}^{3},d^{-3}\log^{-1}d\big\}
, assuming
d_{j}\asymp d
and that
m,\mu,\kappa_{0},r_{j}=O(1)
for simplicity. This constraint is necessary to control the incoherence during the iterations of oRGrad. Additionally, the condition on
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
is required for controlling the higher-order derivatives of the link function.

By choosing a step size such that
\eta\gamma_{\alpha}\asymp C_{m}T^{-1}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}
with a sufficiently large constant
C_{m}>0
, Algorithm
1
produces an estimator
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}
satisfying

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}=O_{m}\bigg(\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}}{T}\bigg),

which matches existing ones
Wang and Li 2020
;
Han et al. 2022b
;
Cai et al. 2022a
in offline binary tensor learning up to logarithmic factors. Theorem
8
requires a sample size of
T=\Omega(d^{3})
, regardless of the dimensionality
m
. This requirement is due to the implicit constraint on the step size
\eta
.

While our result provides the first theoretical guarantee for online binary tensor learning and achieves an optimal error rate under additional conditions, many open problems remain, such as determining the minimal sample size requirement.

5.1
Initialization

The initialization for binary tensor learning is more challenging. Here, we combine a convex optimization method
Davenport et al. 2014
with HOSVD for initialization. Following the notations in Section
3.2.1
, we unfold the tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
into a matrix of balanced dimensions
d_{{\mathcal{I}}}\times d_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}
.

Algorithm 5

Initialization for binary tensor learning

Collect data
\{{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},Y_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T_{1}}
and solve

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{M}}:=\arg\max_{{\bm{M}}}\ L({\bm{M}}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T_{1}}\Big(Y_{t}\log f(\langle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}),{\bm{M}}\rangle)+(1-Y_{t})\log\big(1-f(\langle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}({\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}),{\bm{M}}\rangle)\big)\Big),

s.t.

\displaystyle\|{\bm{M}}\|_{*}\leq\alpha\sqrt{R}\quad{\rm and}\quad\|{\bm{M}}\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\leq\alpha/\sqrt{d^{*}}.

\tilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-1}(\widehat{\bm{M}})
and find
(\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}},\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1},\cdots,\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m})=\textsf{HOSVD}_{{\bm{r}}}(\tilde{\bm{\mathcal{T}}})

Output:
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}=\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}\times_{1}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{1}\cdots\times_{m}\widehat{\bm{U}}_{m}

Theorem 9
.

Suppose Assumption
5
holds, let
c_{m}\in(0,1)
be a small constant, and assume that

\displaystyle\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}\geq(C\alpha L_{\alpha}\gamma^{-3})^{1/2}\max\bigg\{\left(\frac{D_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{2}R\log(D_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}})}{T_{1}^{2}}\right)^{1/4},\left(\frac{RD_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}}{T_{1}}\right)^{1/4}\bigg\},

where
D_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}:=\max\{d_{{\mathcal{I}}},d_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}\}
,
R=\max\{r_{{\mathcal{I}}},r_{{\mathcal{I}}}^{-}\}
, and
C>0
depends only on
c_{m}
. Then, with probability at least
1-D_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-1}
, the output of Algorithm
5
satisfies
\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{m}\gamma_{\alpha}\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}.

6
Sub-Optimal Regret of oRGrad using Constant Step Size

Online learning is particularly useful when an immediate prediction is required upon observing the covariate
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
. Regret performance is crucial for evaluating the prediction accuracy of online algorithms. See
Hazan et al. 2007
,
Zhang 2004
and references therein. Fundamentally, regret measures the difference in prediction performance between an online learner and a static player who has the advantage of hindsight and can make predictions as if all observations were available simultaneously. In the current and upcoming sections, we focus on the regret analysis of the oRGrad algorithm. For simplicity, our analysis is confined to linear cases, including tensor linear regression and noisy tensor completion, i.e., the response satisfies
{\mathbb{E}}Y_{t}=\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\rangle
.

At time
t
, given the current estimate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
, the online learner receives a new observation containing only the covariate information
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
. The learner is then obliged to make a prediction before the true response
Y_{t}
is revealed. In the context of the linear model, a reasonable prediction is
\widehat{Y}_{t}=\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}\rangle
. Prediction accuracy is measured by the difference between
\widehat{Y}_{t}
and the expected response
{\mathbb{E}}Y_{t}
. The regret of oRGrad is thus defined by

{\rm Regret}_{T}({\rm oRGrad})=R_{T}:={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\frac{1}{2}\big(\widehat{Y}_{t}-{\mathbb{E}}Y_{t}\big)^{2}\bigg].

Oftentimes, regret can also be defined as the difference between the cumulative loss incurred by the online learner and that of the best offline player. Specifically, let the loss function be
\ell_{t}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}):=\ell({\bm{\mathcal{T}}},\mathfrak{D}_{t})=(Y_{t}-\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\rangle)^{2}/2
and then the regret of oRGrad can also be defined as

R_{T}:=\inf_{{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\in{\mathbb{M}}_{{\bm{r}}}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}{\mathbb{E}}\big[\ell_{t}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t})-\ell_{t}({\bm{\mathcal{T}}})\big]
. In both linear regression and tensor completion, the infimum is attained at
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}
. Using either definition, and due to the independence between
{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t}
and
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
, one can show that

R_{T}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{\leq t-1}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2},

where
{\mathbb{E}}_{-1}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}=\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}
.
Note that a trivial bound on
R_{T}
is
O(T)
. Online algorithms are deemed effective when the achieved regret is
o(T)
.
The regret performance of oRGrad for both linear regression and tensor completion is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 10
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
2
(respectively, Theorem
6
) hold and assume that the time horizon satisfies
Cm^{1/2}\leq T\leq d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{100m}
for some absolute constant
C>0
. There exists an absolute constant
C_{1}>0
such that the sequence output by oRGrad for online tensor linear regression (online tensor completion, respectively) achieves the regret

R_{T}\leq C_{1}\big(\eta^{-1}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+\eta\cdot\sigma^{2}T\overline{\textsf{dof}}\big).

(7)

The upper bound on the time horizon
T
is due to technical reasons and is relatively weak, as the constant
100
can be replaced by any absolute constant. If the horizon
T
is known, one can set an appropriate step size to minimize the right-hand side of eq. (
7
). By setting
\eta\asymp\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\sigma(T\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}})^{-1/2}
, oRGrad achieves an
O(T^{1/2})
regret or, more precisely,

\displaystyle R_{T}=O\Big(T^{1/2}\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}}^{1/2}\cdot\sigma\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}\Big).

The regret is proportional to the initialization error
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
, implying that oRGrad predicts more accurately if a better initialization is available. Additionally, the regret is proportional to the degree of freedom, reflecting the role played by model complexity.

A square-root regret bound is typical in online learning (see, e.g.,
Zhang 2004
). However, it is well-known in the online learning literature
Hazan et al. 2007
that an optimal regret of
O(\log T)
is attainable when the associated optimization program is convex and the loss function is strongly convex. This logarithmic regret can be achieved by the online gradient descent algorithm with decaying step sizes. In our case, we are restricted to a fixed step size, and it is unclear whether our derived regret of
O(T^{1/2})
is optimal. Interestingly, it matches the recently established regret bound for low-rank bandit learning
(
Shi et al. 2023
)
, although the definition of regret in bandit problem differs from ours. We emphasize that logarithmic regret is attainable even when the time horizon is unknown, provided that adaptive choices of step sizes are allowed—a topic we shall explore in the next section.

Trilemma among computational convergence, statistical error, and regret

Theorems
2
(or Theorem
6
, respectively) and
10
suggest a trilemma in choosing a
constant
step size for online linear regression (or online noisy tensor completion, respectively). If computational convergence is the primary concern, we can choose a large step size
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{comp}}\asymp d^{-1}
, allowing oRGrad to converge within
T=\tilde{O}(d)
iterations. On the other hand, if the goal is to eventually obtain a statistically optimal estimator, we should set a small step size
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{stat}}\asymp T^{-1}
. Lastly, as discussed above, the regret bound in equation (
7
) is minimized when the step size is fixed at
\eta=\eta_{\textsf{regret}}\asymp(Td)^{-1/2}
. It appears that statistical optimality and sharp regret performance cannot be simultaneously achieved with only a fixed step size. Fortunately, we demonstrate in the next section that these two goals can be achieved concurrently by adaptively selecting step sizes.

7
Optimal Adaptive Online Learning when Horizon is Unknown

While online gradient descent with a fixed step size is convenient for algorithm implementation, it has two limitations. First, oRGrad cannot achieve both statistical optimality and sharp regret simultaneously. Second, determining the optimal step sizes,
\eta_{\textsf{stat}}
and
\eta_{\textsf{regret}}
, requires knowledge of the time horizon
T
, which is unrealistic in most applications. To tackle these challenges, we introduce the Adaptive-oRGrad algorithm, which adaptively selects the step sizes. Notably, we demonstrate that Adaptive-oRGrad can achieve both statistical optimality and an optimal
O(\log T)
regret simultaneously, regardless of whether the true time horizon is known. Without loss of generality, we now assume
T
is unknown, but the algorithm and theoretical results are also applicable when the horizon
T
is known. Similarly to Section
6
, we focus on the linear cases, including tensor linear regression and noisy tensor completion.

In the initial phase of the adaptive-oRGrad algorithm, we select a step size
\eta_{0}
and a phase length
t_{0}
. As the algorithm progresses, the step size decreases while the phase length doubles. More precisely, during the
k
-th phase, oRGrad is executed with a step size
\eta=2^{-k}\eta_{0}
for a duration of
2^{k-1}t_{0}
steps. The implementation details of Adaptive-oRGrad algorithm can be found in Algorithm
6
.

Algorithm 6

Adpative-oRGrad –
if time horizon is unknown

Input:
Initial step size
\eta_{0}>0
and initial phase length
t_{0}>0
.

for

k=1,2,3,\ldots

do

Set
\eta_{k}=2^{-k}\eta_{0}

for

t\in\{(2^{k-1}-1)t_{0},\ldots,(2^{k}-1)t_{0}-1\}

do

Run oRGrad with step size
\eta=\eta_{k}

end

for

end

for

The adaptive step size schedule of the Adaptive-oRGrad algorithm is motived by the fact that, for any unknown time horizon
T
, approximately half of the iterations are executed with a step size of order
T^{-1}
. This enables adaptive-oRGrad to deliver a statistically optimal estimator as if the time horizon was known in advance. Notably, an optimal
O(\log T)
regret is also attainable if the initial step size and phase length are carefully chosen. For simplicity, we assume
m,r_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\kappa_{0},\mu_{0}\asymp O(1)
and
d_{j}\asymp d
for all
j\in[m]
. The following theorem characterizes the statistical and regret performance of Adaptive-oRGrad.

Theorem 11
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
2
(Theorem
6
, respectively) hold with the initial step size
\eta_{0}
satisfying the conditions of
\eta
therein. Adaptive-oRGrad algorithm guarantees the following statistical and regret performance for online tensor linear regression (online tensor completion, respectively):

For statsitical optimality.
If
\eta_{0}t_{0}\geq C_{1}
for some absolute constant
C_{1}>0
,
then with probability exceeding
1-16Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
,

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\exp\Big(-c_{1}t_{0}\eta_{0}\log(T/t_{0})\Big)\cdot\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C_{2}t_{0}\eta_{0}\sigma^{2}\cdot\frac{\overline{\textsf{dof}}}{T},

(8)

where
c_{1},C_{2}>0
are some absolute constants.

For optimal regret.
If the initial step size
\eta_{0}=O\big((\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}/\sigma)\cdot(t_{0}\overline{\textsf{dof}})^{-1/2}\big)
and the initial phase length
t_{0}\geq C_{m}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\max\{(\sigma/\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}})^{2}\log^{8}d,(\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}/\sigma)^{2}\}
for some constant
C_{m}>0
depending only on
m
, then

\displaystyle R_{T}=O\Big((t_{0}\overline{\textsf{dof}})^{1/2}\sigma\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}\cdot\log(T/t_{0})\Big).

(9)

By Theorem
11
and by defining
t_{0}\eta_{0}/T=:\tilde{\eta}\geq C_{1}T^{-1}
, the error bound becomes

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\exp\Big(-C_{2}\tilde{\eta}T\log(T/t_{0})\Big)\cdot\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C_{3}\tilde{\eta}\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}}\sigma^{2}.

This should be compared with Theorem
2
(using the inequality
1+x\leq e^{x}
), which states that

\displaystyle\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{T}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\exp(-c_{1}\eta T)\cdot\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C\eta\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}}\sigma^{2}.

This comparison suggests that, by introducing an additional term
\log(T/t_{0})
in the exponent, Adaptive-oRGrad can achieve a faster convergence rate. This is reasonable because, under the adaptive schedule of step sizes, Adaptive-oRGrad chooses more aggressive step sizes except in the final phase, which enforces faster computational convergence. Nevertheless, both algorithms can deliver the statistically optimal rate
\tilde{O}_{p}(\sigma^{2}\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}}/T)
.

Surprisingly, Theorem
11
suggests that the Adaptive-oRGrad algorithm can achieve optimal regret. Suppose that the conditions before eq. (
9
) hold and that the initial phase length is chosen as
t_{0}\asymp\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\max\{(\sigma/\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}})^{2}\log^{8}d,(\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}/\sigma)^{2}\}
. Then, eq. (
9
) yields the following regret bound:

R_{T}=O\Big(\max\{\sigma^{2}\log^{4}d,\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}\}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\log T\Big),

which is exactly (up to the
\log d
factors) the best regret performance achievable when
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}/\sigma\asymp 1
. This optimality holds even if all available information is exploited at each iteration. Indeed, at each time
t
, suppose that
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
is estimated using all the accumulated data before time
t
, i.e., an offline estimate with a sample size of
t
. The minimax optimal error rate in squared Frobenius norm that
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}
can achieve is
O_{p}(\sigma^{2}\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}}/t)

(
Cai et al. 2022a
;
Han et al. 2022b
;
Xia et al. 2021
)
. Consequently, the optimistic accumulated error rate is
{\mathbb{E}}\big[\sum_{t\leq T}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}^{2}\big]\asymp\sigma^{2}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\log T
, dictating a lower bound of
O(\sigma^{2}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\cdot\log T)
for the regret performance.
For general SNR, the regret bound is tight with respect to
T
.
From a theoretical perspective, we can replace
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
in eq. (
9
) with
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
. However, this replacement will affect the choice of the step size
\eta_{0}
since
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}
is unknown.

Finally, it is evident that there are scenarios where the conditions prior to equations (
8
) and (
9
) are simultaneously satisfied. In such cases, the Adaptive-oRGrad algorithm can achieve both statistical optimality and optimal regret.

8
Numerical Experiments and Real Data Analysis

This section presents both numerical simulation results and real data examples. We specifically focus on online tensor linear regression and online tensor completion. Our analysis verifies that the chosen step size effectively balances the trade-off between convergence rate and final error rate. Additionally, we adjust the noise level to evaluate its impact on estimation accuracy.
Throughout this section, we frequently use the relative error, defined by

\textsf{relative error}=\|\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}/\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}},

where
\widehat{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}
is the output of the oRGrad algorithm and
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}
is the unknown tensor. Due to space constraint, one real data example is provided in the Appendix.

8.1
Online tensor linear regression

In this section, we conduct numerical experiments on online tensor linear regression, focusing on the low-rank tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times d\times d}
with
d=30
and Tucker rank
{\bm{r}}=(2,2,2)
. We generate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}
in the factorization form
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}={\bm{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}\times_{1}{\bm{U}}_{1}^{*}\times_{2}{\bm{U}}_{2}^{*}\times_{3}{\bm{U}}_{3}^{*}
, where
{\bm{\mathcal{C}}}^{*}
has independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) entries drawn from
N(0,1)
, and
{\bm{U}}_{1}^{*},{\bm{U}}_{2}^{*},{\bm{U}}_{3}^{*}
have i.i.d. entries drawn from a uniform distribution
\text{Unif}(0,1)
. The corresponding value of
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
is approximately
2.3
.
We use Algorithm
2
for initialization.

Rank Selection

In order to determine the rank
r_{j}
in practice, we may plot the singular values of
\widehat{\bm{N}}_{j}
defined in Algorithm
2
, i.e., the scree plot as shown in Figure
1
. Notably, there is a significant drop after the second-largest singular values across each dimension. More precisely, for each dimension
j\in[m]
, we set
r_{j}=\min\big\{k:\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k}(\sigma_{j^{\prime}})^{2}/\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{d_{j}}(\sigma_{j^{\prime}})^{2}>0.9\big\}
, where
\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{d_{j}}
are the non-increasing eigenvalues of
\widehat{\bm{N}}_{j}
.

(a)

1st dimension

(b)

2nd dimension

(c)

3rd dimension

Figure 1
:
Scree plot for rank selection

In the first experiment, we vary the step size
\eta
among the values
\{5\times 10^{-4},1\times 10^{-3},5\times 10^{-3}\}
while keeping the noise level fixed at
\sigma=1
. The horizon
T
is set to
15,000
. The experimental results are presented in the left panel of Figure
2(a)
. The figure clearly shows that a larger step size (represented by the yellow curve) leads to faster convergence but results in a higher error rate. Conversely, a smaller step size (represented by the blue curve) yields a more accurate estimator, albeit requiring a longer convergence time to reach a stable state. These findings validate our theoretical claims.

(a)

Convergence dynamics of oRGrad for online tensor linear regression (left) and online tensor completion (right) with different step sizes.

(b)

Left: Convergence dynamics of oRGrad for online tensor linear regression with different noise levels; Right: error bar plot based on 10 independent trials for different noise levels.

(c)

Left: Convergence dynamics of oRGrad for online tensor completion with different noise levels; Right: error bar plot based on 10 independent trials for different noise levels.

Figure 2
:
Convergence dynamics of oRGrad for online tensor linear regression and completion.

In the second experiment, we investigate the effect of varying noise levels while keeping the step size fixed at
\eta=1\times 10^{-3}
. To ensure convergence to a stable estimator, we set the horizon
T
to
20,000
steps. The noise level
\sigma
takes on values
\{1,2,3,4,5\}
. For each
\sigma
, we conduct a single convergence dynamics experiment and perform 10 independent trials for the error bar plot. The results are presented in Figure
2(b)
.
From the left panel, we observe that the convergence rates do not significantly vary across different noise levels. However, the right panel reveals a proportional relationship between the final error rate and the noise level, thereby confirming our theoretical findings.

We also present the average per-step runtime as a function of the tensor dimension. We fix the rank
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)^{\prime}
while varying the tensor dimensions
d\times d\times d
for
d\in\{20,25,\ldots,70\}
. For each dimension, we set the horizon to
T=10,000
. The results are displayed in Figure
3
. As shown, the average per-step runtime scales linearly with
d^{3}
, which aligns with the computational cost detailed in Appendix
C
.

Figure 3
:
Average per-step runtime versus cubic of dimension

8.2
Online tensor completion

In this section, we conduct numerical experiments on online tensor completion, focusing on a low-rank tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with dimension
d=75
and Tucker rank
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)^{\prime}
. We generate
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}
by applying the higher-order singular value decomposition (
\textsf{HOSVD}_{{\bm{r}}}
) to a random tensor with entries independently and uniformly drawn from the interval
[0,1]
. The corresponding value of
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
is approximately
0.6
, and the incoherence measure
\mu
is approximately
3.2
. To ensure accurate initialization, we collect data at the beginning of the procedure and employ the second-order moment method proposed in
Xia et al. 2021
.

In the first experiment, we vary the step size
\eta
among the values
\{5\times 10^{-5},7.5\times 10^{-5},1\times 10^{-4}\}
while keeping the noise level fixed at
\sigma=0.1
. The total number of iterations is set to
T=100,000
. The experimental results are depicted in the right panel of Figure
2(a)
. From the figure, we observe that a larger step size (indicated by the yellow curve) leads to faster convergence but also incurs a higher error rate. Conversely, a smaller step size (represented by the blue curve) yields a more accurate estimator at the cost of a longer convergence time to reach a stable state.

In the second experiment, we investigate the impact of varying noise levels while maintaining a fixed step size of
\eta=7.5\times 10^{-5}
. To ensure convergence to a stable estimator, we execute a sufficient number of steps with
T=100,000
. The noise level
\sigma
takes on the values
\{0.025,0.05,0.075,0.1\}
. Convergence dynamics are recorded for each noise level in separate trials, and the error bar plot is based on 10 independent trials. The outcomes are presented in Figure
2(c)
.
From the left panel, it is evident that the convergence rates don’t change across different noise levels. However, the right panel demonstrates a proportional relationship between the final error rate and the noise level, which aligns with our theoretical findings.

8.3
Regret analysis

In this section, we analyze the regret of oRGrad under both constant step size and adaptive settings. We consider a tensor
{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}
with dimension
d=30
and Tucker rank
\mathbf{r}=(2,2,2)
, generated similarly to those in previous sections. The corresponding value of
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
is approximately 0.5, and the noise level is fixed at
\sigma=0.1
throughout the experiment.

We vary the horizon
T
from
5,000
to
200,000
. Following the suggestion in the remark after Theorem
10
, we set the step size to
\eta=\frac{0.01}{\sqrt{T}}
for the constant step size regime. We plot regret versus the square root of the horizon (
\sqrt{T}
) in the left panel of Figure
4
. Additionally, we adopt an adaptive choice of step sizes as suggested by Algorithm
6
, and the resulting regret versus
\log(T)
is plotted in the right panel of Figure
4
. From the figures, we observe that the regret scales linearly with respect to
\sqrt{T}
, aligning with the predictions of Theorem
10
under a constant step size. In the adaptive setting, the regret scales linearly with respect to
\log T
, which significantly improves upon the constant step size regime.

Figure 4
:
Regret performance. Left: a constant step size; Right: adaptive step sizes.

8.4
Real data: Prediction of solar index F10.7

In this section, we target at real-time prediction of solar index F10.7 from ionospheric total electron content (TEC) data. The TEC values derived from multi-frequency Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS) signals and the relevant products have become one of the most utilized parameters in the space weather and ionospheric research community. The F10.7 index measures solar activity based on the radio flux at 10.7 cm (2800 MHz) from the sun’s outer layers. It has been consistently recorded in Canada since 1947 and varies from below 50 to above 300 solar flux units over a solar cycle. Researchers have been investigating how the solar activities as measured by F10.7 and other indices can impact the changes of global TEC maps, see e.g.,
Wang et al. 2023
. However, the relationship between the F10.7 and the TEC maps is still not very clear based on data-driven approaches as shown in
Wang et al. 2023
, though physics implies that these two quantities are highly related to each other. At a particular timestamp, the TEC is measured with a spatial resolution of 1 latitude by 1 longitude, and the temporal cadence is 5 minutes.
The original TEC data from the Madrigal TEC database
(
Rideout and Coster 2006
)
has more than 80% of the data missing on average. We use a completed version of the TEC data
(
Sun et al. 2023
)
using the VISTA algorithm proposed in
(
Sun et al. 2022
)

2
2

2

This dataset is made publicly available in
https://deepblue.lib.umich.edu/data/concern/data_sets/nc580n00z?locale=en
.
(see Figure
5(a)
for an example of one completed measurement).
The response solar index F10.7 is measured hourly
3
3

3

The response can be downloaded from
https://omniweb.gsfc.nasa.gov/form/dx1.html
(see Figure
5(b)
for the solar index in the year 2020).
We use the TEC data and solar flare index in the year 2020. We predict the F10.7 index using the data measured within an hour-long window one day (24 hours) ahead. For example, we predict the F10.7 index at 2 pm on July 20th using the TEC data measured between 1 p.m.- 2 p.m. (12 measurements in total) on July 19th. We down-sample the data in the spatial domain by applying a
6\times 6
maximum kernel to the original dataset. This approach helps reduce redundancy in the original image, emphasizing the active regions with higher values that carry more significant information. Additionally, it enables us to use the offline setting as a benchmark, as it otherwise would require excessive storage.
After pre-processing the data, we obtain 8760 samples, where each covariate sample is of size
16\times 31\times 12
. We adopt the rank selection method mentioned in Section
8.1
, which indicates the rank
{\bm{r}}=(4,4,4)
. We use the first 6000 samples for the initialization; and for the rest of the samples, we perform online prediction.

(a)

Example of one TEC measurement

(b)

Solar index F10.7 in the year 2020

Figure 5
:
Example of one slice in the tensor covariate and the response

Figure 6
:
Predicting solar index F10.7 by online/offline RGrad and comparison with the ground truth (true recording).

As a comparison, we also use the offline-RGrad for our task. We use 3000 samples for initialization and another 3000 samples for refinement, and test the prediction accuracy using the rest of the samples. The results of the prediction are displayed in Figure
6
. And the relative error in prediction and correlations are displayed in Table
2
.

Relative prediction error

Correlation

Online RGrad

0.018

0.988

Offline RGrad

0.085

0.724

Table 2
:
Comparison with offline RGrad in predicting solar index F10.7.

It is clear from both Figure
6
and Table
2
that online RGrad has a better performance and is able to make accurate real-time prediction. From 2020-09-07 to 2020-10-25, it is non-stormy period and both online RGrad and offline RGrad have similar performance. However, there are three stormy periods from 2020-10-26 to the end of 2020. Since the offline RGrad is trained using the data from non-stormy periods, it can not capture the change in covariate and performs poorly in predicting the stormy period. While online RGrad updates the parameter from time to time and is thus able to make accurate predictions even in the stormy periods. This adaptivity of stormy periods is of great importance in space weather monitoring.

Appendix A
Experiment on MovieLens 100k Dataset

We obtained the MovieLens 100k Dataset from the official website
https://grouplens.org/datasets/movielens/
, which comprises 100,000 ratings given by 1000 users for 1700 movies. The data is transformed into a matrix of size
1000\times 1700
with a significant number of missing entries. For our experiment, we utilize the earliest 80,000 ratings as the training data, while the remaining 20,000 ratings serve as the test data.

To address the problem of online matrix completion, we employ the oRGrad algorithm and compare its performance against the offline RGrad method proposed in
Wei et al. 2016
, which serves as a benchmark. We evaluate the results using the mean absolute error (MAE) metric:

\text{MAE}=\frac{1}{|\Omega_{\text{test}}|}\|[\widehat{\bm{M}}-{\bm{M}}^{*}]_{\Omega_{\text{test}}}\|_{\ell_{1}},

where
\Omega_{\text{test}}
is indices of test data and
|\Omega_{\text{test}}|
is the number of test data,
\widehat{\bm{M}}
is the estimator output by either oRGrad or offline RGrad, and
{\bm{M}}^{*}
represents the underlying matrix.
The result are collected in Table
3
.

Although offline RGrad outperforms oRGrad slightly in terms of MAE, it is important to note that offline RGrad is a method that relies on collecting data and lacks the ability to provide timely updates. In contrast, oRGrad sacrifices some accuracy but offers the advantage of providing timely updates without the need for data collection, which can be a time-consuming process.

Appendix B
Tensor logistic regression

Low-rank logistic regression has been studied in
Taki et al.
;
Hung and Wang 2013
;
Shi et al. 2014
. In particular,
Taki et al.
provided a minimax lower bound for matrix logistic regression. In contrast, online low-rank logistic regression has been far less explored in the literature. Similarly as Example
2
, consider the binary observation
Y_{t}\sim\text{Ber}(p_{t})
where
p_{t}=f(\langle{\bm{\mathcal{X}}}_{t},{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\rangle)
and
f:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
is an
inverse link function
. Common choices of
f
include the logistic link
f(\theta)=(1+e^{-\theta/\sigma})^{-1}
and the probit link
f(\theta)=1-\Phi(-\theta/\sigma)
where
\sigma>0
is a scaling parameter. The corresponding loss function is the negative log-likelihood defined as
h(\theta,y)=-y\log(f(\theta))-(1-y)\log(1-f(\theta))
. For any given
\alpha>0
, define

\displaystyle\gamma_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\min\big\{\inf_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}-f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)f(\theta)}{f^{2}(\theta)},\quad\inf_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)(1-f(\theta))+(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}}{(1-f(\theta))^{2}}\big\},

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\max\big\{\sup_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}-f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)f(\theta)}{f^{2}(\theta)},\quad\sup_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{f^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\theta)(1-f(\theta))+(f^{\prime}(\theta))^{2}}{(1-f(\theta))^{2}}\big\}.

and

\displaystyle L_{\alpha}=\sup_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{f^{\prime}(\theta)}{f(\theta)(1-f(\theta))},

which characterizes
\textsf{Err}_{\infty}
and
\textsf{Err}_{2}
. In binary learning problems,
L_{\alpha}
and
\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}/L_{\alpha}
are often regarded as noise level
(
Wang and Li 2020
;
Davenport et al. 2014
;
Cai and Zhou 2013
;
Cai et al. 2022a
;
Han et al. 2022b
)
and the SNR, respectively.

Logistic link

If
f(\theta)=(1+e^{-\theta/\sigma})^{-1}
, we have
f^{\prime}(\theta)=\sigma^{-1}\cdot f(\theta)(1-f(\theta))
and
f^{\prime\prime}(\theta)=\sigma^{-2}\cdot f(\theta)(1-f(\theta))(1-2f(\theta))
. As a result,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\alpha}=\min_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}f(\theta)(1-f(\theta))=\frac{e^{\alpha/\sigma}}{(1+e^{\alpha/\sigma})^{2}\sigma^{2}},\quad{\rm and}\quad\mu_{\alpha}=\max_{|\theta|\leq\alpha}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}f(\theta)(1-f(\theta))=\frac{1}{4\sigma^{2}}.

As
\alpha
becomes larger,
\gamma_{\alpha}
approaches zero exponentially fast. It is therefore typical to assume
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{\ast}\|_{\rm F}
is upper bounded.

Theorem 12
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
3
hold, the initialization satisfies
\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}\leq c_{m}\gamma_{\alpha}\mu_{\alpha}^{-1}\cdot\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}
for some sufficiently small constant
c_{m}>0
,
C_{0}\log d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq\overline{\textsf{dof}}
, the learning rate
\eta
satisfies
\tilde{C}_{1}\eta\overline{\textsf{dof}}\log^{5/2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}\leq 1
with
\tilde{C}_{1}=C_{1}\max\{\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1},\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\mu_{\alpha}^{2},\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\mu_{\alpha}\overline{\textsf{dof}}^{-1}\}
, and the SNR satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{{\textsf{\tiny min}}}^{2}}{L_{\alpha}^{2}}\geq C_{m}\eta\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\overline{\textsf{dof}}\max\{\log^{2}d_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}},\gamma_{\alpha}^{-2}\mu_{\alpha}^{2}\},

where
c_{m},C_{m}>0
depend only on
m
and
C_{0},C_{1}
are absolute constants. Then there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that, for any given horizon
T
, the oRGrad algorithm guarantees that, with probability exceeding
1-14Td_{{\textsf{\tiny max}}}^{-10}
, for all
t\leq T
,

\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{t}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}\leq 2\Big(1-\frac{\eta\gamma_{\alpha}}{8}\Big)^{t}\|{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}_{0}-{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}\|_{\rm{F}}^{2}+C\eta L_{\alpha}^{2}\gamma_{\alpha}^{-1}\cdot\overline{\textsf{dof}}.

Proof of Theorem
12
.

It suffices to validate the conditions of Theorem
1
. Indeed, we have
h_{\theta}(\theta,y)=-yf^{\prime}(\theta)/f(\theta)+(1-y)f^{\prime}(\theta)/(1-f(\theta))
and
